# 1. The Physical Saga Rollback Engine (Compensating Transactions)

### The Problem:
Git Artifacts solves undo for **code, text, and documents** (`git revert`). But autonomous agents perform **real-world side effects**:
* Charging a client via Stripe.
* Dispatching a WhatsApp notification to 200 customers.
* Submitting an EDI customs declaration to the Rotterdam Port Authority.
* Creating a shipping label via DHL.

If a task enters an unrecoverable failure state or the human hits the emergency `STOP` kill switch, **you cannot `git revert` an external API call.**

### The Missing Architecture: The Distributed Saga Engine
Every tool registered in `@naelao/sdk` must define a **Compensating Action**:

```typescript
// packages/sdk/src/types/saga.ts
export interface ReversibleToolDefinition<TParams, TResult, TCompensate> {
  name: string;
  execute: (ctx: ToolContext, params: TParams) => Promise<TResult>;
  
  /** Mandatory Compensating Action for real-world undo */
  compensate: (ctx: ToolContext, compensationPayload: TCompensate) => Promise<void>;
  
  /** Risk classification */
  sideEffectLevel: "IDEMPOTENT_READ" | "SOFT_MUTATION" | "IRREVERSIBLE_PHYSICAL";
}
```

#### Real Implementation Example:
```typescript
export const StripeChargeTool = defineReversibleTool({
  name: "stripe_charge_customer",
  sideEffectLevel: "SOFT_MUTATION",
  
  execute: async (ctx, { customerId, amountCents }) => {
    const charge = await ctx.vault.fetchWithAuth("https://api.stripe.com/v1/charges", {
      method: "POST",
      body: { customer: customerId, amount: amountCents }
    });
    
    // Register Saga rollback payload in SpaceDO.saga_log
    return {
      result: charge,
      compensationData: { chargeId: charge.id, amountCents }
    };
  },

  compensate: async (ctx, { chargeId, amountCents }) => {
    // Automatically called if downstream task fails
    await ctx.vault.fetchWithAuth("https://api.stripe.com/v1/refunds", {
      method: "POST",
      body: { charge: chargeId, reason: "task_aborted" }
    });
    await ctx.thread.postSystemNotice(`🔄 Rolled back Stripe charge ${chargeId} ($${amountCents / 100})`);
  }
});
```

---

# 2. Edge Audio Transcoding (The WASM Codec Gap)

### The Problem:
1. When a user sends a voice note on WhatsApp, Meta delivers audio encoded in **Ogg Opus (`.ogg` container with Opus audio)**.
2. Inbound voice calls via WebRTC / SIP deliver **raw PCM or RTP G.711 / G.722**.
3. **Cloudflare V8 Isolates do NOT have FFmpeg installed.** You cannot execute `child_process.exec("ffmpeg -i input.ogg output.wav")` inside a Durable Object.
4. If an agent tries to pass a raw WhatsApp `.ogg` file to an edge speech-to-text model expecting 16kHz linear WAV PCM, **the transcription crashes immediately**.

```
                               THE AUDIO CODEC PIPELINE
                                          │
    [WhatsApp Voice Note: .ogg (Opus)]   [WebRTC Telephony: G.711 / PCM]
                    │                                    │
                    └──────────────────┬─────────────────┘
                                       │
                                       ▼
             ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
             │ `AudioDecoderDO` (Pure WebAssembly Audio Transcoder)│
             │ • `libopus.wasm` + `libsamplerate.wasm`              │
             │ • Decodes Opus bitstream in-memory in <15ms         │
             │ • Resamples to 16,000 Hz, 16-bit Mono Linear PCM    │
             │ • Zero container / Zero FFmpeg binary requirement   │
             └─────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┘
                                       │
                                       ▼ (Normalized 16kHz PCM Buffer)
             ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
             │ CLOUDFLARE WORKERS AI: WHISPER LARGE-V3             │
             │ Sub-100ms streaming text transcription             │
             └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### The Fix:
AVOS must ship with an in-isolate **WASM Audio Pipeline (`@naelao/audio-wasm`)** compiling `libopus` and a lightweight PCM resampler to WebAssembly. This allows `SpaceDO` to accept any mobile audio format, normalize it to 16kHz WAV in memory in under 20ms, and feed it directly into Whisper without spinning up a Tier 6 container.

---

# 3. The "Day 45 Amnesia" Engine (Hierarchical Sleep Compactor)

### The Problem:
What happens to a Naelao space after **45 days of continuous operations**?
* 1,200 WhatsApp messages.
* 350 completed task branches.
* 40 pull requests merged.
* 15 error incidents resolved.

If you keep appending to SQLite and injecting recent context into prompts:
1. **Context Exhaustion:** The agent's prompt becomes bloated with stale conversational history.
2. **Context Poisoning:** Outdated decisions (e.g., *"We used Stripe in March"*) contradict new decisions (*"We migrated to Adyen in April"*).
3. **Storage Bloat:** SQLite hits memory eviction thresholds during waking hours.

### The Missing Architecture: The 4-Tier Memory Compactor
Memory in Naelao must be structured across four temporal horizons:

```
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                               THE 4-TIER HIERARCHICAL MEMORY LADDER                                    │
├─────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┤
│ Memory Tier         │ Physical Storage Layer   │ Retention Horizon     │ Eviction / Compaction Policy  │
├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
│ **1. Working RAM**  │ In-Memory Context Buffer │ Active Session (<30m) │ Flushed at end of turn.       │
│                     │ (<2,000 tokens)          │                       │                               │
├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
│ **2. Episodic Log** │ `SpaceDO.messages`       │ 7 Days Rolling        │ Nightly Cron summarizes into  │
│                     │ (Relational SQLite)      │                       │ atomic memory rules.          │
├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
│ **3. Semantic Vault**│ Cloudflare Vectorize +   │ Permanent             │ Ingested via RRF (Dense +     │
│                     │ `SpaceDO.rules` (SQLite) │                       │ BM25); retrieved on intent.   │
├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
│ **4. Cold Archive** │ Cloudflare R2            │ Indefinite            │ Full raw logs stored for      │
│                     │ (ZSTD compressed blobs)  │ (Audit Compliance)    │ legal/audit discovery ($0/mo).│
└─────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘
```

#### The Nightly Compactor Daemon (Cloudflare Scheduled Workflow):
Every night at 02:00 local time, a background workflow wakes the `SpaceDO`:
1. Reads all closed threads from the past 24 hours.
2. Extracts **Invariant Lessons** (e.g., *"Client Acme Corp mandates net-60 payment terms"*).
3. Writes lessons to `SpaceDO.rules` and updates Vectorize indexes.
4. Archives the raw message transcripts to Cloudflare R2 and deletes them from SQLite, resetting the operational database to **under 15MB**.

---

# 4. Distributed Agent Tracing & Causality Graphs

### The Problem:
When a human enters a command:
```
"/goal Refactor billing to use Stripe CAS, run tests, notify accounting on WhatsApp"
```
The following distributed cascade fires:
1. `SpaceDO` decomposes intent into 3 tasks in SQLite.
2. Task 1 forks a Git branch and launches 2 leaf workers (Speculative Race).
3. Worker 1 promotes execution to a Tier 6 Container via FUSE to run `npm test`.
4. Worker 2 stalls on an unexpected dependency error.
5. Worker 1 succeeds, triggers an edge webhook to Stripe, and writes a PR card.

**If Task 1 silently hangs at minute 3, where is the bug?**
* Is it a container timeout?
* Is it a FUSE mount deadlock?
* Did Workers AI return a 429?
* Did SQLite throw a locked exception?

Without a unified trace tree, debugging autonomous agents in production is impossible.

### The Fix: Edge-Native OpenTelemetry Causality Graph

```
                                  DISTRIBUTED CAUSALITY TREE
                                               │
             [Root Trace: trace_9a8f10 (Goal: "Stripe CAS Refactor")]
                                               │
                      ┌────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┐
                      ▼                                                 ▼
        [Span 1: SpaceDO.planDAG]                         [Span 2: SpeculativeRace]
        Duration: 340ms | Status: OK                      Duration: 2.1s | Status: OK
                      │                                                 │
                      │                                ┌────────────────┴────────────────┐
                      │                                ▼                                 ▼
                      │                 [Span 2a: Worker_Qwen]           [Span 2b: Worker_Llama]
                      │                 Duration: 1.8s                   Duration: 2.1s (CANCELLED)
                      │                 Tool: Bash("npm test")           Reason: Early-Exit Oracle
                      │                                │
                      ▼                                ▼
        [Span 3: GitCommit (c_9e12)]     [Span 4: Tier6ContainerFUSE]
        Hash: sha256:44b1...             Mount Latency: 14ms
                                         ExitCode: 0
```

* Every agent action, tool invocation, and sub-worker spawn propagates a standard **W3C `traceparent` context header**.
* Spans are emitted asynchronously via Cloudflare Workers Analytics Engine or flushed to an R2-backed **Jaeger / OpenTelemetry lake**, allowing developers in **The Foundry** to visually step through the agent's exact decision tree.

---

# 5. Emerging Market Payment Rails (Beyond Stripe Connect)

### The Problem:
The current spec assumes payouts route through **Stripe Connect**.

If an FDE in Nairobi, Harare, Lagos, São Paulo, or Manila builds a vertical template for local businesses:
* **Stripe does NOT operate in Kenya, Zimbabwe, Nigeria, or 70+ other developing nations.**
* Local businesses pay via **M-Pesa (Kenya), EcoCash (Zimbabwe), Paystack / Flutterwave (Nigeria/Ghana), or Pix (Brazil)**.
* If Naelao only supports Stripe, the entire African, Latin American, and Southeast Asian informal corridor is locked out from buying templates or receiving developer royalties.

### The Fix: Pluggable Multi-Rail Settlement Fabric (`ext_settlement_hub`)

```
                         THE UNIVERSAL SETTLEMENT FABRIC
                                       │
                       Where is the tenant / FDE located?
                                       │
         ┌─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┐
         ▼                             ▼                             ▼
[TIER 1: NORTH AMERICA & EU]   [TIER 2: EMERGING / INFORMAL]  [TIER 3: GLOBAL CRYPTO/BORDERLESS]
• Stripe Connect               • Paystack / Flutterwave       • USDC on Solana / Polygon
• Direct ACH / SEPA Transfers  • M-Pesa Daraja API (Kenya)   • Sub-cent transaction fees
• Credit / Debit Cards         • EcoCash / InnBucks (Zim)     • Instant global settlement
                               • Pix Direct (Brazil)          • Smart Contract Escrows
```

The `blueprint.json` schema decouples currency and payment processor from core billing logic:

```json
{
  "monetization": {
    "supported_processors": ["stripe", "paystack", "mpesa_daraja", "usdc_solana"],
    "base_currency": "USD",
    "accepted_local_currencies": ["KES", "ZWL", "BRL", "NGN", "EUR"],
    "settlement_rail": "fde_preferred"
  }
}
```

---

# 6. Attribute-Based Context Fence (ABAC Prompt Isolation)

### The Problem:
In a multi-user corporate space:
* Alice is the **Chief Human Resources Officer (CHRO)**.
* Bob is a **Junior Backend Engineer**.
* Both have access to the same corporate space and talk to the space's AI agents.

Bob types into the Team Room:
```
"Show me the performance reviews and salary bands for all staff members who joined in Q1."
```

If RAG retrieval operates purely on semantic similarity (Cosine $> 0.80$), **the vector store will retrieve Alice’s confidential compensation files and summarize them for Bob.**

### The Fix: Cryptographic Attribute-Based Access Control (ABAC) in Vectorize

```
[Bob prompts: "Show me all salary bands"]
                   │
                   ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ 1. CALLER CONTEXT INJECTION (Attested Identity)                             │
│    • User: `usr_bob_eng`                                                    │
│    • Clearance Level: 2 (Engineering Staff)                                 │
│    • Department: "ENGINEERING"                                              │
│    • Role Clearance Bitmask: `0b00000010`                                   │
└──────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                   │
                                   ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ 2. VECTORIZE PRE-FILTERING (Hardware-Level SQL/Vector Fence)                │
│    • Query: vector_search(embedding, top_k=10)                              │
│    • HARD FILTER: `WHERE (required_clearance <= 2) AND`                     │
│                   `(department = 'ENGINEERING' OR department = 'PUBLIC')`   │
└──────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                   │
                                   ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ 3. THE RESULT: HR SALARY FILES PHYSICALLY DROPPED AT THE HARDWARE LAYER      │
│    • Even if Cosine Similarity = 0.99, the vector engine never returns      │
│      the chunk to the model's prompt.                                       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Documents are tagged at ingestion with:
* `min_clearance_rank: 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5`
* `department_scope: string[]`
* `allowed_user_ids: string[]` (Optional private override)

The AI model cannot leak what its search queries are mathematically prevented from retrieving.

---

# 7. Offline-First Local Sync (CRDT Edge-to-Device Sync)

### The Problem:
Lawyers work on airplanes. Doctors work in hospital basements with concrete walls. Cargo truckers drive through dead-zones in transit corridors.

Currently, Naelao assumes a live WebSocket connection to `SpaceDO` on Cloudflare Anycast. When connectivity drops, **the desktop canvas freezes, and user input is blocked.**

### The Fix: Local-First Embedded SQLite with CRDT Reconciliation

```
  [OFFLINE CLIENT DEVICE: Laptop / Tablet]               [CLOUD: SpaceDO on Cloudflare]
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐                ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Local In-Browser SQLite (`wa-sqlite`)│                │ Authoritative DO SQLite    │
  │ • User continues editing contract    │                │ • Background agents continue│
  │ • Local commits signed with Ed25519  │                │   running tasks            │
  └──────────────────┬───────────────────┘                └─────────────┬──────────────┘
                     │                                                  │
                     └──────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────┘
                                        ▼            ▼
                                 [RECONNECTED TO INTERNET]
                                        │
                                        ▼
                         ┌─────────────────────────────┐
                         │ CRDT / AST 3-WAY RECONCILER │
                         │ • Replays local operational │
                         │   intents against SQLite log│
                         │ • Resolves conflicts without│
                         │   data loss                 │
                         └─────────────────────────────┘
```

1. The desktop web app (`apps/shell`) embeds a local WebAssembly SQLite engine (`wa-sqlite` / CR-SQLite) backed by IndexedDB.
2. When disconnected, edits are written to the local database and cryptographically signed by the user's local session key.
3. Upon reconnection, the client flushes its signed mutation log to `SpaceDO`. The DO applies an **Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) 3-Way Merge**, resolving conflicts deterministically without overwriting concurrent background agent commits.

---

# 8. Institutional Meta-Learning (The Self-Distillation Loop)

### The Problem:
AI models do not possess long-term procedural memory.
* An agent tries to compile a module using `npm run build`, fails because Node 20 deprecated an old OpenSSL flag, and spends 4 turns figuring out the fix.
* Three days later, another worker runs into the same OpenSSL error on a different branch and **spends the same 4 turns and burns the same tokens discovering the exact same fix.**

The platform lacks an **automated institutional memory loop**.

### The Fix: The Automated `POSTMORTEM.md` and Rule Compiler

```
                       THE INSTITUTIONAL META-LEARNING LOOP
                                         │
                   [Worker resolves an error after 2+ retries]
                                         │
                                         ▼
                   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────┐
                   │ 1. INCIDENT EXTRACTION SENTRY             │
                   │ System 1 JEV evaluates the resolution:    │
                   │ Did the fix uncover a non-obvious rule?   │
                   └─────────────────────┬─────────────────────┘
                                         │
                                         ▼ (Yes: High Novelty Score)
                   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────┐
                   │ 2. ATOMIC LESSON SYNTHESIS                │
                   │ Generates concise 2-line invariant:       │
                   │ "When building Module X, always pass      │
                   │  NODE_OPTIONS='--openssl-legacy-provider'"│
                   └─────────────────────┬─────────────────────┘
                                         │
                                         ▼
                   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────┐
                   │ 3. COMMIT TO `/.avos/INSTITUTIONAL_MEMORY`│
                   │ • Ingested into project's Vectorize index │
                   │ • Future sub-planners pre-load this rule  │
                   │   automatically during task decomposition │
                   └───────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Every time an error is overcome, the workspace compiles that lesson into its permanent institutional memory. Across months of operation, the workspace becomes **progressively faster, cheaper, and more resilient**, creating a defensible operational moat.

---

# Summary of What Must Be Built Next

To transition Naelao / AVOS from architectural maturity into an unassailable enterprise and consumer standard, the immediate engineering roadmap must tackle these 8 components:

```
┌────┬─────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ #  │ Missing Component               │ Primary Implementation Deliverable                     │
├────┼─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ 1  │ **Physical Saga Rollbacks**     │ `compensate()` hooks in `@naelao/sdk` for real-world undo│
│ 2  │ **WASM Audio Transcoding**      │ `@naelao/audio-wasm` (Ogg/Opus $\to$ 16kHz PCM decoder) │
│ 3  │ **Hierarchical Sleep Compactor**│ Cloudflare Scheduled Workflow running nightly log sweeps│
│ 4  │ **Agent Causality Tracing**     │ Edge OpenTelemetry collector with traceparent propagation│
│ 5  │ **Emerging Settlement Rails**   │ M-Pesa, Paystack, Pix, and USDC settlement plugins     │
│ 6  │ **ABAC Context Fencing**        │ Hardware-level bitmask filtering on Cloudflare Vectorize│
│ 7  │ **Offline-First Sync Engine**   │ `wa-sqlite` client storage + AST 3-way reconciler      │
│ 8  │ **Institutional Meta-Learning** │ Automated `POSTMORTEM.md` incident harvester in SQLite │
└────┴─────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Addressing these eight gaps provides the complete systems substrate for Naelao: an operating system capable of resilient, sovereign, and self-correcting autonomous operations across any enterprise or emerging-market corridor.

To build, compile, and test every single subsystem of **Naelao / AVOS** locally on your PC—from the Zig-WASM Git mesh and Durable Object SQLite engines to Tier-6 containers, WebAssembly audio codecs, and local sovereign AI—your development machine must be configured as an **Edge-and-Systems Workstation**.

Because Cloudflare’s local development stack runs on **`workerd`** (the open-source C++ runtime that powers Cloudflare Workers globally), **you can simulate 98% of Cloudflare’s entire infrastructure (Durable Objects, SQLite, R2, Workflows, Queues, Vectorize) completely offline on your PC.**

Here is the complete blueprint to set up your PC so everything is production-ready.

---

# 1. Hardware & OS Baseline

### Minimum vs. Recommended PC Specs
* **RAM:** **16 GB minimum**; **32 GB recommended** (Running Docker + Miniflare `workerd` + Local Ollama LLM + IDE will consume 18–22 GB).
* **Storage:** **50 GB free NVMe SSD** (Docker images, WASM compilation caches, and local model weights).
* **CPU:** 6+ Cores (AMD Ryzen 5/7/9, Intel Core i5/i7/i9, or Apple Silicon M-series).
* **GPU (Optional but recommended):** NVIDIA RTX (8GB+ VRAM) or Apple Silicon Unified Memory if you want to test the **Subsystem 8 Sovereign Local AI / Ollama Air-Gap mode** without burning cloud API credits.

### The Operating System Rule:
* **If you are on Windows:** **Do NOT develop directly in native Windows PowerShell.** POSIX file paths, symlinks, FUSE mounts, and WASM compilers will break. You **must** install **WSL2 (Windows Subsystem for Linux)** running **Ubuntu 24.04 LTS**.
* **If you are on macOS or Linux:** Native terminal is fully supported out of the box.

---

# 2. Step-by-Step Installation Guide (WSL2 / Linux / Mac)

Open your terminal (WSL2 Ubuntu terminal on Windows) and run the following setups:

---

### Step A: Core JavaScript & Edge Package Tooling

Do not use standard `npm`. Use **`pnpm`** (for strict workspace isolation across your monorepo) and **Node.js 22 LTS**.

```bash
# 1. Install Node Version Manager (nvm)
curl -o- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nvm-sh/nvm/v0.40.0/install.sh | bash
source ~/.bashrc

# 2. Install Node.js 22 LTS
nvm install 22
nvm use 22

# 3. Enable Corepack & PNPM (Fastest, strictly isolated monorepo manager)
corepack enable
corepack prepare pnpm@latest --activate

# 4. Install Cloudflare Wrangler globally (Runs workerd locally)
pnpm add -g wrangler
```

Verify:
```bash
node -v      # v22.x.x
pnpm -v      # v9.x.x or higher
wrangler -v  # wrangler 3.x+
```

---

### Step B: The WASM Compilers (Zig & Rust)

Naelao requires **Zig** (for the Git Mesh `@naelao/git-wasm`) and **Rust** (for WASM audio transcoding, AST parsers, and the bridge daemon).

```bash
# 1. Install Rust & WASM compilation targets
curl --proto '=https' --tlsv1.2 -sSf https://sh.rustup.rs | sh -s -- -y
source "$HOME/.cargo/env"

# Add WASM and WASI compilation targets
rustup target add wasm32-unknown-unknown
rustup target add wasm32-wasip1

# Install wasm-pack and binaryen (wasm-opt for compressing binaries)
cargo install wasm-pack
sudo apt update && sudo apt install -y binaryen

# 2. Install Zig (v0.13.0+) for the Zig-to-WASM Git Mesh
sudo apt install -y snapd
sudo snap install zig --classic --beta
```

Verify:
```bash
rustc --version  # rustc 1.80+
zig version      # 0.13.0 or higher
wasm-opt --version
```

---

### Step C: Tier-6 Container Runtime (Docker)

To test the **Tier-6 Container Ladder** (`ToolSandboxDO` and FUSE Artifact mounting), your PC needs Docker running with rootless container privileges:

```bash
# If on Ubuntu / WSL2: Install Docker Engine
sudo apt install -y docker.io docker-compose-v2
sudo usermod -aG docker $USER
newgrp docker

# Test Docker non-root execution
docker run hello-world
```
*(If on Windows with WSL2, install **Docker Desktop** on Windows and enable "Use the WSL 2 based engine" in Settings $\to$ Resources $\to$ WSL Integration).*

---

### Step D: Audio & Codec Fixture Tools

Even though production runs WASM audio decoders in-isolate, your dev PC needs native **FFmpeg** to inspect, generate, and verify WhatsApp `.ogg` test audio files:

```bash
sudo apt install -y ffmpeg libopus-dev
```

---

### Step E: Sovereign Local AI (Ollama)

To test Subsystem 8 (Air-Gapped Space Compliance Lock where the agent routes prompts to local hardware instead of cloud AI):

```bash
# 1. Install Ollama locally
curl -fsSL https://ollama.com/install.sh | sh

# 2. Pull lightweight models for local testing
ollama pull llama3.2:3b         # Ultra-fast 3B model for local tests
ollama pull qwen2.5-coder:7b    # Coding model for leaf worker tests
ollama pull nomic-embed-text    # Embedding model for local vector tests
```

---

### Step F: Inbound Webhook Tunnels (`cloudflared`)

To test WhatsApp webhooks, Twilio telephony, or Stripe webhooks on your local PC **without deploying to the cloud**, install Cloudflare Tunnel (`cloudflared`). It gives your PC a secure, free public HTTPS URL:

```bash
# Install Cloudflare Tunnel
curl -L --output cloudflared.deb https://github.com/cloudflare/cloudflared/releases/latest/download/cloudflared-linux-amd64.deb
sudo dpkg -i cloudflared.deb
rm cloudflared.deb
```

---

# 3. Monorepo Repository Structure

Organize your Naelao codebase on your PC as a single monorepo:

```text
naelao-workspace/
├── apps/
│   ├── shell/                  # React/Vite Desktop Shell (Canvas, Chat, HUD)
│   └── whatsapp-gateway/       # Anycast Ingress Worker (Webhooks & Sessions)
├── packages/
│   ├── sdk/                    # @naelao/sdk (Public Extension & Worker SDK)
│   ├── git-wasm/               # Zig source code compiling to git_engine.wasm
│   ├── audio-wasm/             # Rust source compiling to opus_decoder.wasm
│   ├── concurrency/            # ConcurrencyDO & Node Mutex Lease engine
│   └── writeguard/             # AST SQL and Bash command security sentry
├── workers/
│   ├── core-space/             # SpaceDO (Root Tenant Engine + SQLite VFS)
│   ├── outbound-vault/         # Outbound Vault Proxy Worker (TLS token injector)
│   └── local-tunnel/           # LocalTunnelDO (Reverse WebSocket bridge)
├── pnpm-workspace.yaml
├── package.json
└── wrangler.toml               # Unified local workerd development manifest
```

---

# 4. The Master Local Development Configuration (`wrangler.toml`)

This configuration instructs **Miniflare 3 / `workerd`** to simulate the entire Cloudflare Edge on your PC, including **Durable Objects with SQLite, R2 Storage, Queues, and Workflows**:

```toml
name = "naelao-local-core"
main = "workers/core-space/src/index.ts"
compatibility_date = "2026-10-01"
compatibility_flags = ["nodejs_compat"]

# 1. LOCAL DURABLE OBJECTS WITH SQLITE
[durable_objects]
bindings = [
  { name = "SPACE_DO", class_name = "SpaceDO" },
  { name = "CONCURRENCY_DO", class_name = "ConcurrencyDO" },
  { name = "TOOL_SANDBOX_DO", class_name = "ToolSandboxDO" },
  { name = "LOCAL_TUNNEL_DO", class_name = "LocalTunnelDO" }
]

[[migrations]]
tag = "v1"
new_sqlite_classes = ["SpaceDO", "ConcurrencyDO"]

# 2. LOCAL R2 STORAGE EMULATION (Pointer-LFS)
[[r2_buckets]]
binding = "ARTIFACTS_BUCKET"
bucket_name = "naelao-local-artifacts"

# 3. LOCAL QUEUES EMULATION (Webhook Bursts)
[[queues.producers]]
binding = "WEBHOOK_QUEUE"
queue = "inbound-webhook-buffer"

# 4. SERVICE BINDINGS (Zero-Leak Vault Interception)
[[services]]
binding = "VAULT_PROXY"
service = "outbound-vault-worker"

# 5. ENVIRONMENT VARIABLES
[vars]
ENVIRONMENT = "local_development"
LOCAL_OLLAMA_ENDPOINT = "http://127.0.0.1:11434"
```

# 5. The Pre-Flight Golden Test Script

Create a script named `scripts/verify-pc-setup.sh` in your project root to verify that your PC has every compiler, runtime, and emulator ready:

```bash
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -e

echo "=== 🔍 RUNNING NAELAO PC PRE-FLIGHT VERIFICATION ==="

# 1. Check Runtimes
echo -n "Checking Node.js & PNPM... "
node -v > /dev/null && pnpm -v > /dev/null && echo "✅ OK"

echo -n "Checking Wrangler (workerd engine)... "
wrangler -v > /dev/null && echo "✅ OK"

# 2. Check Compilers
echo -n "Checking Zig Compiler (Git WASM Engine)... "
zig version > /dev/null && echo "✅ OK"

echo -n "Checking Rust & WASM Targets... "
rustc --version > /dev/null
rustup target list | grep "wasm32-unknown-unknown (installed)" > /dev/null && echo "✅ OK"

# 3. Check Docker
echo -n "Checking Docker (Tier 6 Containers)... "
docker info > /dev/null 2>&1 && echo "✅ OK"

# 4. Check Local AI
echo -n "Checking Ollama (Air-Gapped Sovereign AI)... "
curl -s http://127.0.0.1:11434/api/tags > /dev/null && echo "✅ OK"

# 5. Check Cloudflare Tunnel
echo -n "Checking cloudflared (Webhook Ingress)... "
cloudflared -v > /dev/null && echo "✅ OK"

echo "======================================================"
echo "🚀 PC IS FULLY CONFIGURED FOR NAELAO LOCAL DEVELOPMENT"
echo "======================================================"
```

Make it executable and run it:
```bash
chmod +x scripts/verify-pc-setup.sh
./scripts/verify-pc-setup.sh
```

# 6. Your Daily Development Commands

Once configured, here is how you run and test Naelao end-to-end on your PC:

1. **Start the local Cloudflare Edge engine (DOs, SQLite, R2):**
   ```bash
   pnpm wrangler dev --port 8787
   ```
2. **Expose your local engine to real WhatsApp / Stripe webhooks:**
   ```bash
   cloudflared tunnel --url http://localhost:8787
   ```
   *(Copy the generated `https://xxxx.trycloudflare.com` URL into your Meta WhatsApp App dashboard).*
3. **Run your automated 6-tier persistence test suite:**
   ```bash
   pnpm vitest run tests/ladder_persistence.test.ts
   ```
4. **Compile the Zig Git Mesh to WASM:**
   ```bash
   cd packages/git-wasm && zig build -Dtarget=wasm32-freestanding -Doptimize=ReleaseSmall
   ```

With this setup, your PC has the **exact same primitives, memory isolation, and execution capabilities** as Cloudflare’s global network. You can develop, break, and test the entire operating system locally before deploying to production.

NOTE: OUR MACHINE CANT RUN  OLLAMA MODELS YET SO WE WILL USE CLOUD MODELS LIKE GEMIN AND GROQ



To stress test an autonomous edge operating system like **Naelao / AVOS**, running basic unit tests is not enough. You have **Durable Object single-thread write queues, WebSockets holding live Canvas connections, Zig-WASM Git memory allocations, and WhatsApp webhook bursts** hitting the edge simultaneously.

If you onboard 100 active businesses without stress testing, your system will not fail with a clean error; it will fail with **SQLite lock timeouts, WebSocket dropped frames, and memory crashes.**

Here is the complete blueprint to run **Full End-to-End (E2E) Integration Tests and high-volume Stress Tests using Grafana k6**, right from your PC.

# Phase 1: The Golden Rule (The Zero-Cost Edge LLM Mock)

**Never stress test against real frontier LLMs (OpenAI, Anthropic, or paid Workers AI).**  
If you simulate 500 concurrent virtual users generating tasks, you will burn **$1,500 in API credits in 4 minutes** or hit provider rate limits immediately.

Before firing k6, configure your local environment to route LLM requests to an **Internal Mock AI Responder (`workers/mock-ai/src/index.ts`)**:

```typescript
// workers/mock-ai/src/index.ts
// Emulates streaming tokens and instant tool calls with realistic 150ms latency
export default {
  async fetch(req: Request): Promise<Response> {
    const { messages, tools } = await req.json() as any;

    // Simulate 120ms non-autoregressive decision latency
    await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, 120));

    // Return deterministic mock tool call for load testing
    return new Response(JSON.stringify({
      id: "mock_turn_" + crypto.randomUUID(),
      model: "mock-fast-edge",
      choices: [{
        message: {
          role: "assistant",
          content: null,
          tool_calls: [{
            id: "call_123",
            type: "function",
            function: {
              name: "Bash",
              arguments: JSON.stringify({ cmd: "echo 'stress test pass'" })
            }
          }]
        }
      }],
      usage: { prompt_tokens: 450, completion_tokens: 38, total_tokens: 488 }
    }), {
      headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" }
    });
  }
};
```

# Phase 2: Installing Grafana k6 on Your PC

**Grafana k6** is a load-testing engine written in Go. Unlike tools written in Node.js, k6 uses almost zero CPU and can easily simulate **thousands of concurrent connections, HTTP/2 multiplexed streams, and persistent WebSockets** from a single development PC.

### Installation (WSL2 Ubuntu / Linux / Mac):

```bash
# Ubuntu / Debian / WSL2
sudo gpg -k
sudo gpg --no-default-keyring --keyring /usr/share/keyrings/k6-archive-keyring.gpg --keyserver hkp://keyserver.ubuntu.com:80 --recv-keys C5AD17C747E3415A3642D57D77C6C491D6AC1D69
echo "deb......



To ensure you are never surprised by production bugs, memory leaks, or race conditions when onboarding real users, you need a **Dual-Engine Verification & Stress Architecture**:

```
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

├──────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Testing Layer                        │ Tools & Purpose                                                 │
├──────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **1. Protocol & Load Stress Engine** │ **Grafana k6 (Protocol VUs):** 500–2,000 Virtual Users hitting  │

```

Here are the concrete scripts, configurations, and procedures to stress-test your entire stack on your development PC before deploying to production.

---

# 1. Installing the Testing Toolchain on Your PC

In your WSL2/Linux terminal:

```bash
# 1. Install Grafana k6 (The industry standard load testing engine)
sudo gpg -k
sudo gpg --no-default-keyring --keyring /usr/share/keyrings/k6-archive-keyring.gpg --keyserver hkp://keyserver.ubuntu.com:80 --recv-keys C5AD17C747E3415A3642D57D77C6C491D6AC1D69
echo "deb.....


To ensure you are never blindsided by production bugs, you cannot rely on basic unit tests. Naelao involves **distributed state machines, WebSocket streams, concurrent SQLite transactions, WASM memory heaps, and multi-agent speculative races**. 

To break your system *before* users do, you need a **4-Tier Industrial Testing & Stress Suite**:





**Yes, absolutely.** In fact, using **Jev (or open-source System 1 models like Laya)** for browser control is one of the most significant breakthroughs in autonomous agents. 

The exact reasons developers use Jev to play Minecraft, Doom, and Subway Surfers—**sub-50ms reaction times, zero hallucinations, non-autoregressive parallel choice selection, and single-pass calibrated probabilities**—make it mathematically superior to traditional LLMs for browser automation.

---

### 1. The Core Problem with Conventional Browser Agents

Traditional browser agents (such as standard LangChain, Claude Computer Use, or raw GPT-4o) treat web navigation like a conversational dialogue:

```
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                        THE SLOW AUTOREGRESSIVE BROWSER LOOP                            │
├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ 1. Capture 1280x800 screenshot (1,600 vision tokens) or 20,000 tokens of raw HTML.     │
│ 2. Send to a massive 70B–400B parameter frontier model.                                │
│ 3. Wait 3 to 7 seconds while the model generates verbose tokens word-by-word.          │
│ 4. Parse the output text, hoping it didn't mess up the JSON coordinate schema.         │
│ 5. Execute 1 click.                                                                    │
│ 6. Repeat 15 times $\to$ **Total Task: 90 seconds, $0.75 burned, 40% failure rate.**   │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

When someone uses a web application (booking a flight, filling an EDI customs form, updating a CRM), **they don't need a conversational essay; they need a reflex.** 

---

### 2. How the Game Loop Translates to the Browser

When people use Jev or train a System 1 policy to play games, the loop looks like this:
$$\text{Game State (Pixels/Entities)} \longrightarrow \text{System 1} \longrightarrow \text{Action (Jump, Turn, Attack)}$$

In a web browser, the "game" is the **Accessibility Tree (AXTree)**, and the "controller buttons" are the interactive DOM elements:

```
                               THE SYSTEM 1 BROWSER FLYWHEEL
                                             │
                                             ▼
                         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                         │ TIER 4: CLOUDFLARE KITESURF (<30MB Edge DOM) │
                         │ • Strips CSS/styling/images                  │
                         │ • Extracts Accessibility Tree (AXTree)       │
                         │ • Numbers every interactive element [1..N]   │
                         │ • Latency: sub-150ms                         │
                         └──────────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                                                │
                                                ▼ (Indexed State Table: <300 tokens)
                         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                         │ SYSTEM 1 JEV (Non-Autoregressive Decision)   │
                         │ Questions asked simultaneously in 1 pass:    │
                         │ 1. `operation`: [CLICK, SELECT, TYPE, SCROLL]│
                         │ 2. `target_element`: [Element #1 ... #N]     │
                         │ 3. `is_goal_reached`: [TRUE, FALSE]          │
                         │ • Latency: **30ms – 70ms** | Cost: $0.00004  │
                         └──────────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                                                │
                         ┌──────────────────────┴──────────────────────┐
                         ▼ (If operation == CLICK / SCROLL)            ▼ (Only if operation == TYPE_TEXT)
          ┌─────────────────────────────┐               ┌─────────────────────────────┐
          │ ZERO-LLM INSTANT EXECUTION  │               │ CALL LIGHTWEIGHT SYSTEM 2   │
          │ Kitesurf dispatches CDP     │               │ Fast 3B/7B model generates  │
          │ click in 5ms. Loops next turn.              │ the exact string to type.   │
          └─────────────────────────────┘               └─────────────────────────────┘
```

In early implementations of this pattern (such as Gregor Zunic's `browser-use/jev-ultrafast` and `laya-browser`), **an agent searches and books flights on Google Flights in 7.1 seconds for $0.0039 total cost**. The same flow using Claude 3.5 Sonnet takes 45+ seconds and costs over $0.40.

---

### 3. How to Structure Jev for Browser Use in Naelao

In Naelao's runtime hypervisor, web perception runs across **Tier 4 (Cloudflare Kitesurf)** and **Tier 5 (Headless Chromium)**. 

Instead of passing the browser viewport to a frontier LLM, `NaelaoAgentDO` runs the **System 1 Fast Decision Loop**:

```typescript
// packages/runtime/src/browser/SystemOneBrowserDriver.ts
import { KitesurfSession } from "@naelao/kitesurf";
import { JevClient } from "@naelao/jev";

export interface BrowserStepResult {
  completed: boolean;
  actionTaken: string;
  durationMs: number;
}

export class SystemOneBrowserDriver {
  constructor(
    private browser: KitesurfSession,
    private jev: JevClient
  ) {}

  async executeStep(userGoal: string): Promise<BrowserStepResult> {
    const start = performance.now();

    // 1. Kitesurf extracts clean, numbered interactive AXTree in 80ms
    const { indexedElements, stateSummary } = await this.browser.getIndexedAXTree();

    // Example indexedElements passed to Jev:
    // [1] Input: "Departure Airport" (value: "")
    // [2] Input: "Arrival Airport" (value: "")
    // [3] Button: "Search Flights"
    // [4] Checkbox: "Non-stop only"

    // 2. Query Jev with 3 typed questions simultaneously in a single forward pass (<60ms)
    const decision = await this.jev.predict({
      state: `GOAL: ${userGoal}\nCURRENT URL: ${await this.browser.getUrl()}\nPAGE ELEMENTS:\n${stateSummary}`,
      questions: {
        operation: {
          type: "choice",
          choices: {
            CLICK: "Click an interactive button, link, or tab",
            TYPE_TEXT: "Focus an input field to enter text",
            SCROLL_DOWN: "Scroll page down to find more content",
            GOAL_FINISHED: "The requested information is visible or task completed",
            BLOCKED: "Encountered CAPTCHA or unrecoverable error"
          }
        },
        targetId: {
          type: "choice",
          // Maps dynamically to the visible element numbers [1..N]
          choices: indexedElements.reduce((acc, el) => {
            acc[`el_${el.id}`] = `${el.tag} "${el.label || el.text}"`;
            return acc;
          }, {} as Record<string, string>)
        },
        confidence: {
          type: "score",
          scale: ["uncertain", "likely", "certain"]
        }
      }
    });

    const selectedOp = decision.answers.operation.choice;
    const targetElementId = decision.answers.targetId.choice.replace("el_", "");

    // 3. Fast-Path Physical Execution
    if (selectedOp === "CLICK") {
      await this.browser.click(`[data-naelao-id="${targetElementId}"]`);
    } else if (selectedOp === "SCROLL_DOWN") {
      await this.browser.scroll(0, 600);
    } else if (selectedOp === "TYPE_TEXT") {
      // Determine what to type: either extracted directly from goal, or via micro-LLM
      const textToType = extractParamFromGoal(userGoal, indexedElements[targetElementId]);
      await this.browser.type(`[data-naelao-id="${targetElementId}"]`, textToType);
    }

    return {
      completed: selectedOp === "GOAL_FINISHED",
      actionTaken: `${selectedOp} on element #${targetElementId}`,
      durationMs: performance.now() - start
    };
  }
}
```

---

### 4. Active Learning: Making the System 1 Agent "Learn the Game" of Target Websites

Just like players train reinforcement learning (RL) models to master video game mechanics, an enterprise or FDE can train a System 1 browser agent to **master internal web portals** (like SAP, Salesforce, or national customs terminals).

```
                     THE WEBGYM ACTIVE LEARNING PIPELINE
                                      │
                                      ▼
               [PHASE 1: THE HUMAN FDE RECORDING SESSION]
      • FDE performs a customs clearance or invoice lookup ONCE.
      • The browser daemon logs the state-action trajectory:
        $(s_0, a_0) \to (s_1, a_1) \to \dots \to (s_k, \text{GOAL})$
                                      │
                                      ▼
               [PHASE 2: SYNTHETIC PERTURBATION (WebGym)]
      • The FDE exports the target website HTML into a sandbox.
      • A script generates 1,000 synthetic permutations:
        - Different cargo numbers
        - Dynamic button positions
        - Pop-up banners / cookie warnings
                                      │
                                      ▼
               [PHASE 3: REINFORCEMENT LEARNING FOR DECISIONS (RLCD)]
      • Fine-tunes an open-source System 1 model (like `laya-browser` / ModernBERT)
        using policy gradient rewards:
        +1.0 for task completion
        -0.05 per unnecessary click/step
        -1.0 for invalid form submission
                                      │
                                      ▼
               [PHASE 4: SUPERHUMAN SPEED DEPLOYMENT]
      • Deploys as an ultra-compact 320M parameter WASM/ONNX model
      • Runs inside Cloudflare Workers or directly inside the browser tab
      • Executes complex 10-step enterprise workflows in under 2 seconds.
```

---

### 5. Benchmark Comparison: LLM vs. Jev System 1 Browser Control

```
┌─────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐

│                             │ (Claude 3.5 / GPT-4o)     │ + Cloudflare Kitesurf         │
├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
│ **Step Latency**            │ 3,000 ms – 6,000 ms       │ **35 ms – 80 ms**             │
├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
│ **10-Step Workflow Time**   │ 45 to 90 seconds          │ **1.8 to 4.2 seconds**        │
├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
│ **Cost per 1,000 Steps**    │ ~$25.00 – $50.00          │ **$0.042 (1,000x cheaper)**   │
├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
│ **Format Errors (JSON)**    │ 2% to 5% schema failure   │ **0.00% (By construction)**   │
├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
│ **Bandwidth / Memory**      │ High (Massive Screenshots)│ Low (<300 token text AXTree)  │
├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
│ **Execution Environment**   │ Requires GPU cloud cluster│ Runs inside V8 Isolate / Edge │
└─────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘
```

---

### 6. Where This Sits in Naelao's Architecture

In Naelao, this is not a separate application; it is the **fast-path implementation of the `Browser(action, url)` primitive**:

1. **System 2 (The Planner):** Emits high-level intent:  
   `"Log into Rotterdam Port Authority and download the discharge manifest for MSKU-908124."`
2. **System 1 (Jev / Laya in Tier 4 Kitesurf):** Takes over the interactive loop. It clicks, fills fields, selects dropdowns, and clicks download in **1.4 seconds** without prompting System 2 on every intermediate click.
3. **The Result:** The downloaded PDF is written directly to DO SQLite VFS and Git Artifacts, and System 2 resumes architectural work.

Using **Jev System 1 for browser execution** eliminates the latency and high costs that plague traditional AI web automation, transforming slow web crawlers into **real-time, reflex-driven browser operators.**

The short answer is: **yes, it is a genuine breakthrough, but only if you use it for what it was actually built to do.**

Since TypeSafe AI launched Jev, developers, hackathon teams, and browser-agent builders (like *Browser Use*, *Browserbase*, *Stagehand*, and *Retriever AI*) have stress-tested it across thousands of real production workflows. 

Real-world testing reveals clear strengths alongside critical limitations and failure modes.

---

### 1. Where Testers Are Blown Away (The Good)

#### A. The "Flight Booking" Benchmark (Browser-Use)
In the open-source community, Gregor Zunic and the *Browser Use* team tested Jev on complex, multi-step browser tasks:
* **Claude 3.5 Sonnet / Computer Use:** Took **45 to 60 seconds** and cost **~$0.45** to navigate Google Flights, search a route, select departure/return dates, and filter non-stop flights.
* **Jev Ultrafast:** Completed the exact same sequence in **7.1 seconds for $0.0039 total cost (over 100x cheaper and 6x faster)**.
* **Why it worked:** It didn't pause for 5 seconds on each click to reason about what an airport is; it simply received the accessibility tree (AXTree), selected element `#14` via a single non-autoregressive forward pass, and triggered the click.

#### B. Zero Schema Drift and Zero Markdown Fences
Every developer who has built browser agents with GPT-4o or Claude knows the pain of:
* The LLM wrapping its JSON in ` ```json ... ``` `.
* The LLM hallucinating an invalid enum (e.g., returning `"click_button"` when your code expects `"CLICK"`).
* The LLM writing an apology (*"I'm sorry, I cannot find the button..."*) instead of returning coordinates.

In Jev, because the question schema is defined in code before inference, **schema failure rate in production is 0.00%**. It physically cannot output malformed data or hallucinate text.

#### C. Real Calibrated Probabilities (Not Fake LLM Confidence)
Standard LLMs are notoriously uncalibrated: when an LLM is wrong, it is still 99% confident in its hallucination. 

Jev was trained using **Reinforcement Learning for Calibrated Decisions (RLCD)**. Testers (like Bryo AI and Vercel AI Gateway teams) found that when Jev returns a `0.95` probability, it is genuinely accurate ~95% of the time. When it returns `0.52`, it is genuinely guessing between two ambiguous elements. This allows software harnesses to execute high-confidence actions autonomously while cleanly escalating low-confidence edge cases to a human or a reasoning model.

---

### 2. Where Testers Got Burned (The Bad & The Limitations)

Despite the hype, developers who treated Jev like a "magic drop-in replacement for Claude" hit major walls:

#### Trap 1: It Has Zero Chain-of-Thought (No Scratchpad)
Jev evaluates state in a **single forward pass**. It has no internal reasoning tokens, no scratchpad, and no deliberation time:
* If a website requires deduction (e.g., *"Find the cheapest flight, but only on an airline that allows 2 free checked bags, factoring in a 3-hour layover restriction"*), **Jev fails completely.**
* It cannot hold multiple constraint variables in its head and solve a puzzle.
* **The Rule:** Jev is for **reflexes and candidate selection**, not complex multi-step logical deduction.

#### Trap 2: The "State Projection" Garbage-In-Garbage-Out Trap
As early browser testers noted (e.g., *Failproof AI*):
* If your accessibility tree extractor cuts off the page or numbers the DOM incorrectly, Jev cannot "guess" what is missing.
* A frontier LLM looking at a screenshot can often infer: *"Oh, the button is partially obscured behind that cookie banner, let me dismiss the banner first."*
* Jev only knows the exact structured text table you hand it. If your DOM scraper is dirty, Jev will execute the wrong choice with high speed.

#### Trap 3: Armin Ronacher's Warning (Delegating Hallucinations)
Armin Ronacher (creator of Flask/Sentry) pointed out an important architectural reality:
> *"The design delegates the hallucination problem a little bit to the user, who has to decide whether a 50% probability is worth acting on."*

Jev guarantees valid types, but **it does not guarantee correct human intentions**. If opposing elements look identical (e.g. two buttons labeled "Cancel" on a modal), your code must explicitly inspect the returned probability distribution and know how to branch if it is uncertain.

#### Trap 4: Network Roundtrips vs. Model Latency
While TypeSafe AI advertises 20ms–50ms model inference, early access testers using the cloud API over public internet measured **120ms to 250ms end-to-end latency** due to standard TLS and HTTP round-trips. (Still dramatically faster than an LLM's 3,000ms–5,000ms, but not instant unless running at the edge or locally).

---

### 3. The Consensus: How Production Teams Actually Use It

The industry has settled on a clear design pattern: **The Two-Brain Browser Architecture**.

```
                           THE TESTED TWO-BRAIN BROWSER PATTERN
                                            │
                                            ▼
                           [User Goal: "Book 2pm dental checkup"]
                                            │
                                            ▼
                       ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
                       │ 🧠 SYSTEM 2 (Reasoning LLM: Claude/o3)   │
                       │ Decides high-level plan & constraints:   │
                       │ • Target date: Tomorrow                  │
                       │ • Doctor: Dr. Smith                      │
                       │ • Price limit: $150                      │
                       └────────────────────┬─────────────────────┘
                                            │ (Hands structured goal to browser loop)
                                            ▼
                       ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
                       │ ⚡ SYSTEM 1 (Jev / Fast Reflex Sentry)   │
                       │ Runs at 20 FPS inside the browser:       │
                       │ 1. Click "Appointments" (99.8% conf)     │
                       │ 2. Click "Tomorrow" (98.2% conf)         │
                       │ 3. Select "2:00 PM" (97.4% conf)         │
                       │ 4. Click "Proceed to Checkout"           │
                       └────────────────────┬─────────────────────┘
                                            │
                          Did Jev confidence drop below 0.80?
                          OR does text require creative generation?
                                            │
                             ┌──────────────┴──────────────┐
                             ▼ YES                         ▼ NO
              ┌─────────────────────────────┐ ┌─────────────────────────────┐
              │ WAKE UP SYSTEM 2            │ │ EXECUTE IMMEDIATELY         │
              │ Solve ambiguous edge case   │ │ Fast reflex (<70ms)         │
              └─────────────────────────────┘ └─────────────────────────────┘
```

---

### 4. What This Means for Naelao / AVOS

Using Jev (or edge/open alternatives like Cloudflare Clef or open-weight deciders) for **Tier 4 Kitesurf Browser Use** is highly viable:

1. **It makes web tasks viable for $6.99 consumer plans:** You can run 200 browser clicks for fractions of a cent, whereas 200 clicks on Claude Computer Use costs $10.00+ and bankrupts your unit economics.
2. **It eliminates UI lag:** Web forms, dropdowns, and button sequences execute in 1 to 3 seconds total instead of taking over a minute.
3. **The Implementation Rule:** You must keep your System 2 planner in charge of **overall goal verification**, and use System 1 strictly as the **DOM click/fill reflex operator**. If confidence dips below `0.80`, pause and let the planner re-orient.


**You just hit on the architectural evolution from "chat agents" to a true distributed operating system.**

In traditional agent systems (LangChain, CrewAI, AutoGen), every single worker is assumed to be a **generative LLM**. That is why swarms are so slow, expensive, and fragile: you have five 70B models all having a conversation with each other, burning hundreds of seconds and dollars on basic mechanical tasks.

A worker in Naelao does not have to be a chatty generative thinker. A worker can be a **System 1 Reflex Worker (Policy Agent)** powered entirely by Jev or lightweight non-autoregressive encoders.

---

### 1. Thinking Workers (System 2) vs. Reflex Workers (System 1)

```
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                         THE TWO CLASSES OF NAELAO WORKERS                              │
├─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┤
│ Dimension           │ 🧠 Thinking Worker (System 2)   │ ⚡ Reflex Worker (System 1)    │
├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Engine**          │ Frontier / Edge LLM (Claude/Qwen)│ Jev / Laya (Non-autoregressive)│
├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Latency**         │ 1,500ms – 5,000ms per step      │ **30ms – 70ms per step**       │
├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Cost**            │ ~$0.01 – $0.05 per call         │ **$0.00004 per call**          │
├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Cognitive Role**  │ Synthesis, code generation,     │ Deterministic choices, triage, │
│                     │ architectural planning, nuance  │ DOM navigation, test refereeing│
├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Communication**   │ Conversational natural language │ Typed Enums, Scores, and Booleans│
└─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘
```

---

### 2. Packaging Jev as Type 1 (Prebuilt) vs. Type 2 (Custom)

```
                                  JEV AS A WORKER PRIMITIVE
                                              │
         ┌────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┐
         ▼                                                                         ▼
┌──────────────────────────────────┐                      ┌──────────────────────────────────┐
│ TYPE 1: PREBUILT SYSTEM WORKERS  │                      │ TYPE 2: CUSTOM DOMAIN WORKERS    │
│ (`NaelaoAgentDO` Fast Actors)    │                      │ (Cloudflare Dynamic Workflows)   │
├──────────────────────────────────┤                      ├──────────────────────────────────┤
│ • Baked into core Naelao image   │                      │ • Built by FDEs via `@naelao/sdk`│
│ • Runs in-memory V8 (<50ms RPC)  │                      │ • Runs in Workflows sandboxes    │
│ • Universal platform utilities   │                      │ • Bound to fine-tuned JEV heads  │
│ • Examples: BrowserReflexWorker, │                      │ • Examples: CustomsEDIValidator, │
│   CommandFirewall, InboxTriage   │                      │   OncologySymptomSentry, KYC     │
└──────────────────────────────────┘                      └──────────────────────────────────┘
```

#### How Jev Operates as a Type 1 Prebuilt Worker:
* **`worker_browser_reflex`:** Sits directly behind the `Browser` tool. The Planner delegates a goal (*"Download invoice from Stripe"*); the worker fires 12 DOM clicks in **800ms total** and returns the downloaded PDF pointer to the Planner.
* **`worker_command_firewall` (WriteGuard):** Sits behind `Bash`. Inspects every raw terminal and SQL command in 40ms, blocking destructive patterns (`DROP TABLE`, `rm -rf /`) before the hypervisor executes them.

#### How Jev Operates as a Type 2 Custom Worker:
* An FDE creates a **specialized vertical reflex worker** deployed as a Cloudflare Workflow.
* The worker is coupled with a **domain-fine-tuned Jev head** (e.g. trained on 500 historical customs declarations or medical triage guidelines).
* It executes high-velocity, high-stakes verification without invoking expensive third-party LLMs.

---

### 3. Six Jev-Powered Reflex Workers Beyond Browser Use

Browser and desktop automation are only the beginning. Jev-powered System 1 workers can automate critical platform mechanics across the Naelao operating system:

```
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                              6 POWERFUL JEV REFLEX WORKER ARCHETYPES                                   │
├──────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Worker Archetype                     │ Operational Specification & Mission                             │
├──────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ 1. **The Ingress Triage Sentry**     │ 50ms categorization of incoming WhatsApp voice notes, emails,   │
│    (`worker_inbox_triage`)           │ and webhooks. Routes to the exact subspace/project in 1 step.   │
├──────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ 2. **The Test Oracle & Loop Referee**│ Inspects error stacks and compiler logs in 40ms. Diagnoses if   │
│    (`worker_oracle_referee`)         │ an error is a flaky test, network timeout, or logic bug.        │
├──────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ 3. **The AST Merge Conflict Arbiter**│ When parallel workers produce competing diffs, Jev evaluates the│
│    (`worker_ast_arbiter`)            │ syntactic trees and chooses the winner without hallucination.   │
├──────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ 4. **The Form & EDI Schema Inspector**│ Rapidly validates 200 fields in a customs manifest or loan doc  │
│    (`worker_edi_inspector`)          │ against statutory schemas in 60ms for pennies.                  │
├──────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ 5. **The Ledger Drawdown RiskGuard** │ Evaluates outbound payments against mathematical velocity rules │
│    (`worker_financial_riskguard`)    │ and triggers human circuit breakers before capital moves.       │
├──────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ 6. **The Context Filter / ABAC Sentry│ Hardware-level vector pre-filtering; validates whether caller    │
│    (`worker_context_fence`)          │ has clearance to view retrieved chunks before prompt injection. │
└──────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

---

#### Archetype 1: The Test Oracle & Loop Referee (`worker_oracle_referee`)
In our Speculative Race (SR-EEO) protocol, multiple leaf workers race to solve an objective.
* Instead of burning 5 seconds asking Claude 3.7 *"Did this vitest test pass or fail, and what caused the error?"*, the Orchestrator hands the stdout to the **Jev Oracle Referee**:

```typescript
const verdict = await jevReferee.predict({
  state: `TEST RUN STDOUT:\n${testOutput}\nEXIT CODE: ${exitCode}`,
  questions: {
    status: {
      type: "choice",
      choices: {
        ALL_GREEN: "All test assertions passed cleanly",
        ASSERTION_FAILURE: "Code logic produced incorrect output",
        SYNTAX_COMPILATION_ERROR: "Code failed to compile or import broken",
        TRANSIENT_NETWORK_FLAKE: "External DB or fetch timed out (safe to retry)",
        INFINITE_LOOP_TIMEOUT: "Execution exceeded time limit"
      }
    },
    action: {
      type: "choice",
      choices: {
        MERGE_PR: "Accept branch immediately",
        RETRY_WORKER: "Pass error stack back to same worker",
        TERMINATE_AND_ESCALATE: "Halt task and notify human review"
      }
    }
  }
});
```
* **Latency:** **45ms**. **Cost:** **$0.00004**.
* The Orchestrator knows instantly whether to merge, retry, or flag a human.

---

#### Archetype 2: The Ingress Triage Sentry (`worker_inbox_triage`)
When hundreds of audio notes, photos, and messages burst through the Naelao WhatsApp gateway:
* You cannot afford to run Claude or GPT-4o on every *"Hi,"* *"Thanks,"* or photo upload.
* The **Jev Ingress Triage Sentry** processes the raw transcript in **60ms**:
  * Is this an invoice? $\to$ Route to `Subspace: Accounting`.
  * Is this a port container issue? $\to$ Route to `Subspace: Logistics`.
  * Is this an emergency medical symptom? $\to$ Trigger **STAT Human Call Alarm**.
* The heavy System 2 models are never awakened unless a real generative synthesis task is demanded.

---

#### Archetype 3: The AST Merge Conflict Arbiter (`worker_ast_arbiter`)
When multiple agents or humans make concurrent changes:
* Traditional Git uses dumb line-by-line diffs (`<<<<<<< HEAD`), which fail when two edits modify the same function safely.
* The **Jev AST Arbiter** parses the two AST trees:
  * Selects whether Node A and Node B can be merged safely or if there is a semantic collision.
  * Resolves non-conflicting imports, variable additions, and type annotations in **30ms**.

---

### 5. How Developers Define a Jev Reflex Worker in `@naelao/sdk`

Here is how an FDE or vertical software builder defines a custom Jev Reflex Worker:

```typescript
// extensions/ext_logistics/src/workers/PortReflexWorker.ts
import { defineReflexWorker, ReflexContext } from "@naelao/sdk";

export const PortReflexWorker = defineReflexWorker({
  id: "worker_port_reflex",
  name: "Maritime Port Operations Reflex Worker",
  role: "OPERATOR",
  runtimeType: "FAST_ISOLATE_ACTOR", // Sub-50ms execution in V8

  // 1. BOUND CAPABILITIES
  capabilities: ["Browser", "Read"],

  // 2. THE JEV DECISION MATRIX
  reflexPolicy: {
    evaluateStep: async (ctx: ReflexContext, currentSnapshot: string) => {
      // Evaluates current terminal or browser state non-autoregressively
      return await ctx.jev.predict({
        state: currentSnapshot,
        questions: {
          nextAction: {
            type: "choice",
            choices: {
              DISCHARGE_CONTAINER: "Discharge event verified in manifest",
              HOLD_INSPECTION: "Customs hold flag detected on cargo",
              RETRY_MANIFEST: "Network or portal session timed out",
              COMPLETE: "Workflow objectives met"
            }
          },
          urgencyScore: {
            type: "score",
            scale: ["routine", "priority", "emergency"]
          }
        }
      });
    }
  }
});
```

---

### Summary: Why This Changes Everything

1. **Breaks the LLM Cost & Latency Ceiling:**  
   Instead of every subagent burning 5 seconds and $0.03 per step, your fleet of Jev Reflex Workers executes in **40ms for $0.00004**.
2. **Eliminates Hallucination from Deterministic Steps:**  
   Browser clicks, test verification, command firewalls, and document validations are handled by mathematically calibrated choices, not generative text streams.
3. **True Swarm Specialization:**  
   Your swarms consist of a small number of **System 2 Thinkers** (planners, architects, generative coders) orchestrating a fast army of **System 1 Reflex Workers** (browsers, linters, triage sentries, oracles).

This is what makes Naelao's execution ladder capable of running complex swarms at **$2.10/user/month with institutional 92%+ gross margins.**

```
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                   THE 4-TIER HARDENING TEST HARNESS                                    │
├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ 1. 🎭 BROWSER E2E (Playwright): End-to-end user journeys (Chat ↔ Canvas ↔ Terminal ↔ Ghost Diffs).     │
├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ 2. ⚡ K6 CONCURRENCY & RACE ENGINE: Mass virtual user simulation (Task CAS, WebSockets, Git locks).   │
├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤

├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤

In your WSL2/Linux terminal:

```bash

sudo gpg --no-default-keyring --keyring /usr/share/keyrings/k6-archive-keyring.gpg --keyserver hkp://keyserver.ubuntu.com:80 --recv-keys C5AD17C747E3415A3642D57D77C6C491D6AC1D69
echo "deb




Yes, you can absolutely use Jev AI for browser use, and developers are already doing it. Because Jev is a System 1 decision model built by TypeSafe AI, it excels at ultra-fast, cheap, and probabilistic decisions—making it perfect for rapidly deciding what to click or where to navigate next on a webpage. [1, 2, 3, 4, 5] 
However, because Jev cannot generate free-form text, you have to build your system a bit differently than a standard game-playing agent. [5, 6] 
## How to Build a Jev-Powered Browser Agent
To make a "System 1" browser runner, you must implement a hybrid architecture: [1, 6] 

[Webpage HTML/DOM] ──> [Deterministic Code] ──> [Jev AI (System 1)]
                             │                      │ (Fast Click/Nav Decision)
                             ▼                      ▼
                     [Frontier LLM] ──────────> [Browser Action (Playwright)]
                  (For Text Input/Forms)


   1. The HTML Parser (The "Eyes"): Use deterministic code or a lightweight script to scrape the DOM or accessibility tree of the page. This compiles a structured list of interactive elements (buttons, links). [5, 7, 8] 
   2. Jev AI (The "Reflexes"): You pass the state of the page to Jev using a Choice or Score question type. For example: "Given these 5 visible buttons, which one brings us closer to purchasing the item?" Jev will return the exact button index in milliseconds. [1, 9, 10] 
   3. The Frontier LLM (The "Brain" - Hybrid Layer): Whenever your browser agent encounters a text field (like typing a custom search query or filling out a form), Jev cannot do it. Your code must hand that specific task off to a traditional LLM (like Claude or GPT) to write the text, then pass control back to Jev. [5, 6] 
   4. The Execution Layer: Use an automation framework like [Playwright](https://playwright.dev/) or Puppeteer to actually execute the clicks and keystrokes on a Chromium browser. [5, 7] 

## Current Tools & Integrations
If you don't want to build it entirely from scratch, you can look into existing tools that have already mapped Jev to browser usage:

* Browserbase: Kyle Jeong from [Browserbase](https://www.browserbase.com/) has actively integrated Jev to power browser use agents for fractions of a cent.
* rtrvr.ai: A browser agent tool that features an experimental "Try Jev" switch to offload action-scoring and next-click decisions directly to the Jev model.
* LangChain: You can use [LangChain's Jev Harness](https://www.langchain.com/blog/building-a-harness-with-jev) to orchestrate the routing between your browser state, Jev, and your main LLM framework. [3, 11] 

## Why this works so well for Browsing
While websites don't require the sub-100ms response times that twitch-reflex video games do, using Jev drops your browser automation costs drastically. Instead of feeding massive amounts of HTML into a costly LLM for every single click, Jev evaluates the structural options at $0.042 per million input tokens with free output tokens, cutting your browser agent operating costs by up to 90%. [1, 2, 5] 
Are you trying to build a custom browser script from scratch (using python/node), or are you looking to plug Jev into an existing framework like Browser Use or LangChain? Let me know your stack so we can map out the code structure.

What Cody Schneider built is one of the clearest demonstrations of **GTM (Go-to-Market) Engineering** in production. 

Most people use AI in marketing for low-leverage tasks like drafting a generic tweet or rewriting an email. Schneider’s agent achieved a drop from an $1,100 cost-per-lead (CPL) to $250 by running an **autonomous, closed-loop software engine that self-optimizes daily** [1, 2].

Building this as a first-class project template in Naelao (**`tpl_autonomous_ads_engine`** or **`ext_growth_core`**) requires understanding how the loop actually operates under the hood and how to map it to Naelao's primitives.

### 1. Dissecting the Strategy: Why Did CPL Drop from $1,100 to $250?

The drop wasn't driven by "better ad copy prompts." It was driven by closing **three structural leaks** that drain Google Ads budgets:

```
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                        THE 3 CRITICAL BUDGET LEAKS CLOSED                              │
├─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┤
│ The Budget Leak     │ How Normal Marketers Do It      │ How the Autonomous Agent Did It│
├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
│ **1. Search Term**  │ Marketers check Search Terms    │ **Continuous Negative Mining:**│
│ **Budget Bleed**    │ once a month. Google Broad Match│ Agent pulls raw query reports  │
│                     │ burns 40–70% of spend on junk.  │ daily; flags junk in minutes.  │
├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
│ **2. Quality Score**│ Send all traffic to 1 generic   │ **Programmatic 1:1 Pages:**    │
│ **Disconnect**      │ homepage. Google penalizes ad   │ Spins up custom landing pages  │
│                     │ rank because copy doesn't match.│ matching exact search queries. │
├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
│ **3. Static Rules** │ Bids and rules set manually     │ **Self-Modifying Logic:**      │
│ **Stagnation**      │ in Google Ads UI dashboards.    │ Agent updates its own filtering│
│                     │                                 │ code based on real CRM revenue.│
└─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘
```

#### Leak 1: The Search Terms Report vs. The Keyword List
In Google Ads, the keywords you bid on are **not** what users actually type. 
If you target *"enterprise compliance software"*, Google's Broad Match will show your ad for *"free compliance software reddit"*, *"compliance officer salary"*, or *"login to compliance portal"*. 
* Each click costs $15 to $80.
* Normal marketing teams clean this monthly.
* **The Agent:** Pulls the Search Terms report daily. It evaluates every query that spent money without converting, identifies non-commercial intent, and immediately injects it into Google Ads as an exact-match **Negative Keyword**. **It stops the cash bleed automatically**.

#### Leak 2: 1:1 Query-to-Page Hyper-Relevance
Google calculates ad pricing using **Quality Score (1 to 10)** based on:
$$\text{Quality Score} = \text{Ad Copy Relevance} \times \text{Expected CTR} \times \text{Landing Page Experience}$$
If a user searches *"HIPAA compliance software for dental clinics in Texas"*:
* **Old way:** Ad says *"Compliance Software"* $\to$ links to `company.com` (Quality score: 4/10 $\to$ high CPC, low conversion).
* **The Agent way:** Ad says *"Texas Dental Clinic HIPAA Compliance"* $\to$ dynamically generates and serves `company.com/l/texas-dental-hipaa` with tailored copy and local customer logos (Quality score: 9/10 $\to$ 50% cheaper clicks, 3x higher conversion).

### 3. Step 1: Ingestion & The Fast Negative Keyword Sentry

Evaluating 2,000 search terms using Claude 3.7 or GPT-4o would take 5 minutes and cost $10 per night. 

Instead, Naelao uses **System 1 (Jev / Laya)**. It screens queries non-autoregressively at **35ms per query for fractions of a cent**:

```typescript
// extensions/ext_growth_core/src/NegativeKeywordSentry.ts
import { JevClient } from "@naelao/jev";
import { GoogleAdsClient } from "./google_ads_api";

export async function processSearchTermsReport(
  jev: JevClient, 
  adsApi: GoogleAdsClient,
  reportRows: Array<{ query: string; spend: number; conversions: number }>
) {
  const negativesToAdd: string[] = [];

  for (const row of reportRows) {
    // Only evaluate queries that burned cash without conversions
    if (row.spend > 15.0 && row.conversions === 0) {
      
      // Fast System 1 classification (<35ms)
      const verdict = await jev.predict({
        state: `SEARCH QUERY: "${row.query}" | SPEND: $${row.spend}`,
        questions: {
          intent: {
            type: "choice",
            instructions: "Determine if this query has commercial intent to buy our B2B compliance software.",
            criteria: {
              HIGH_BUYING_INTENT: "Direct commercial intent (pricing, buy, demo, software, vendor)",
              JOB_SEEKER_OR_RESEARCH: "Looking for jobs, salary, templates, definitions, or homework",
              EXISTING_USER_LOGIN: "Looking to login, sign in, portal, support, or documentation",
              COMPETITOR_FREE_SEEKER: "Searching for free, open source, crack, or torrent alternatives"
            }
          }
        }
      });

      const selected = verdict.answers.intent.choice;
      if (selected !== "HIGH_BUYING_INTENT") {
        negativesToAdd.push(row.query);
      }
    }
  }

  // Push negative keyword list back to Google Ads Campaign in 1 batch call
  if (negativesToAdd.length > 0) {
    await adsApi.addNegativeKeywords(negativesToAdd);
  }

  return { addedCount: negativesToAdd.length, savedMonthlyBurnEst: negativesToAdd.length * 45 };
}
```
